### 139. DEVIL → CEO (round 28)

The weakest point is the CEO's early-warning design. Its thresholds cannot detect the failure mode the evidence actually shows, so 'month 12 go/no-go' is not a mitigation test.

- **Massachusetts would not have tripped signals 1-3 in time.** The MWRA ended Biobot testing at the end of July 2026 as the state moved toward in-house testing (biobot_public_contracts_customers_and_06, 2026-07). That was a state-level decision to replace the program. It was not a renewal discount request or a cadence cut. The signals key on buyer behaviour inside the account. Substitution decisions are made in budget and procurement processes the vendor may not see until the contract expires.
- **Signal 2 is set too loose for a 75% price gap.** It flags failure only if more than 20% of renewals (about 7 of 33) ask for discounts above 15%. The CEO's own maths says an in-house alternative costs about 25% of Biobot's price. A buyer facing that gap is more likely to leave outright than to ask for 15% off, as Massachusetts did. My inference: the threshold measures the wrong response.
- **Signal 4 tolerates losing 4 of the 9 grant-funded renewals.** That is about 12% of the 33-renewal cohort, gone before 'failing' is declared at month 12. Combined with the existing $280k churn, that already breaches the 95% GRR milestone on the CFO's average-contract basis.
- **Signal 1 has a size problem too.** It allows up to 2 in-house RFPs in 6 months before 'failing'. One Massachusetts-scale loss was about 47% of opening ARR. My inference: the trigger should weight accounts by ARR, not count them, and contract-level ARR is not in evidence.
- **The 2024 precedent was already a signal.** The CDC program was followed by a Verily award (biobot_public_business_and_market_03, 2024-05-01). That shows substitution had happened at the largest buyer two years earlier, so a 12-month wait to call it 'structural' is unjustified.

The proposed dashboard would most likely report 'green' right up until another large contract expires.

**Maths** (✅ checks out): `20% x 33 = 6.6, so about 7 renewals; 9 - 5 = 4 grant lapses tolerated; 4 / 33 = 12% of cohort; $10,200,000 / 70 = $145,714 avg ARR; 4 x $145,714 = $582,856; ($10,200,000 - $280,000 - $582,856) / $10,200,000 = 91.5% GRR; $400,000 x 12 = $4,800,000; $4,800,000 / $10,200,000 = 47%`

**Assumptions:**

- All operating figures ($10.2m opening ARR, $280k churn, 70 programs, 9 of 33 grant-funded renewals) are synthetic scenario data from earlier turns.
- Lapsed contracts carry average ARR of $145,714; contract-level ARR is not in evidence.
- Inference: buyers facing a 4x cost gap exit rather than negotiate modest discounts.
- Inference: substitution decisions are often not visible to the vendor before contract expiry.

**Cites:** `biobot_public_contracts_customers_and_06` — Biobot public data and source ledger (public sources, checked 2026-10-22); `biobot_public_business_and_market_03` — Biobot public data and source ledger (public sources, checked 2026-10-22)

- ❔ _unverifiable_: Signal 4 tolerates losing 4 of the 9 grant-funded renewals before declaring failure at month 12. — Signal 4 thresholds not in evidence.
- ✅ _supported_: 9 minus 5 equals 4 grant lapses tolerated. — Arithmetic verified in code; its inputs are the speaker's own
- ✅ _supported_: 4 lost renewals is about 12% of the 33-renewal cohort. — 4/33 ≈ 12.1%.
- ✅ _supported_: Average ARR per contract is $10,200,000 / 70 = $145,714. — Arithmetic verified in code; its inputs are the speaker's own
- ❔ _unverifiable_: Losing 4 average contracts equals $582,856 of ARR. — No valid supporting citation
- ❔ _unverifiable_: With $280k existing churn plus 4 lapsed average contracts, GRR would be 91.5%, breaching the 95% GRR milestone. — 91.5% math holds, but the 95% GRR milestone is not in evidence.
- ❔ _unverifiable_: Signal 1 allows up to 2 in-house RFPs in 6 months before declaring failure. — Signal 1 not in evidence.
- ✅ _supported_: The Massachusetts contract at $400,000 per month equals $4,800,000 per year, about 47% of $10.2m opening ARR. — Arithmetic consistent with sources.
- ❔ _unverifiable_: The CDC wastewater program was followed by a Verily award in 2024. — A later Verily award is recorded, but its 2024 date is unclear.
- ✅ _supported_: The MWRA ended Biobot testing at the end of July 2026 as the state moved toward in-house testing. — MWRA notice states this.
- ✅ _supported_: The Massachusetts decision was a state-level decision to replace the program, not a renewal discount request or a cadence cut. — State ended Biobot testing, moving to an in-house replacement program.
- ❔ _unverifiable_: Signal 2 flags failure only if more than 20% of renewals (about 7 of 33) ask for discounts above 15%. — Signal definitions not in evidence.
- ✅ _supported_: 20% of 33 renewals is 6.6, about 7 renewals. — 33 renewals; arithmetic correct.

## VC decision

**pass** — conviction 62%

| Rubric | Score | Why |
|---|---:|---|
| Team | 3/5 | The founders have credible origins. Ghaeli managed Underworlds and owned the government interface, Matus co-led the project, and Ghaeli's strength was relationships with mayors. In 2017 Matus named a business-side hiring gap. There is no evidence on the current leadership roster, who owns state renewals, or whether the founders are still in role. The 70 FTEs at FY2025 against an unverified Growjo figure of 33 employees is an unresolved red flag. Uncertainty is high. |
| Market | 2/5 | The only sizing input is a synthetic $43.32M planning scenario, and it describes itself as not observed demand or a TAM. The top-down figures were dropped as unverifiable. Buyers do include pharma and public-health appropriations. However, philanthropically funded WastewaterSCAN, universities and public labs supply the same signal, which compresses what buyers will pay. |
| Product | 3/5 | The 886-site wastewater surveillance network has dashboards. Biobot markets forecasting and substance-use intelligence beyond COVID and runs substance-use programs. Nothing shows any customer paying for analytics separately from testing, or any measured forecast accuracy. The CDC role went to Verily, which offers lab testing, analytics and sequencing. |
| Traction | 3/5 | The synthetic book shows $14.384M contracted ARR across 89 programs. ARR-to-ARR growth was about 41% in nine months, gross retention 97.3%, net retention 113.9%, and logo renewal roughly 90-94% across cohorts. Real wins include ONDCP in 2026 and HHS in 2021. Several things cap the score: the data is synthetic, and the $280K of churn cannot reconcile with the ended $400K/month Massachusetts contract. Also, only 24 of 35 due contracts renewed on a dedicated budget line, with 9 of 33 renewals on time-limited grants. |
| Moat | 2/5 | The one patent covers upstream sampling hardware and gives no exclusivity over surveillance. In 2017 the founders were told they had no defensible IP and chose to differentiate on analytics. Massachusetts moved in-house at an expected one-quarter of the cost, and MWRA still publishes the historical data, so the data history did not lock the customer in. The CDC moved to Verily. The network and procurement incumbency are the only partial barriers. |
| Timing | 2/5 | Post-COVID, the largest federal role has moved to Verily, and a flagship state has insourced. Government contract concentration and in-house substitution are flagged risks. Grant funding underpins part of the renewals. The 2026 ONDCP contract shows some non-infectious-disease demand, but the timing trend currently runs against vendor-run testing. |
| Business Model | 3/5 | Run-rate service gross margin is 57.4%, on about $676 per scheduled sample against $288 of cost. Burn is $336,630/month against $12.8M of cash, about 38 months of runway. These figures are synthetic, on scheduled rather than delivered volume, and not GAAP. Sales cycles run 163 to 318 days, and some paid pilots close without converting. |

**For:**

- $14.384M contracted ARR across 89 programs, with about 41% ARR-to-ARR growth in nine months (synthetic).
- Synthetic gross retention of 97.3%, net retention of 113.9%, and due-cohort logo renewal of about 90-94% from FY2023 to 2026.
- Healthy unit economics: 57.4% run-rate gross margin, and lab cost would need to rise about 2.6x to break even.
- About 38 months of runway at current burn, so the company is not raising to survive.
- Real recent wins (ONDCP 2026) and a substance-use product line outside COVID infectious-disease testing.

**Against:**

- Demonstrated substitution. Massachusetts ended a reported $400K/month contract for in-house testing at an expected one-quarter of the cost, and the CDC national role went to Verily.
- The data does not reconcile. A $4.8M/yr public contract value cannot fit inside $280K of synthetic churn, so the true ARR base and its concentration are unknown.
- Thin defensibility: a narrow hardware patent, no assay or model IP, and free alternatives such as WastewaterSCAN and public labs.
- Grant dependence. 9 of 33 renewals ran on time-limited grants, and only 24 of 35 due contracts renewed on a dedicated budget line.
- The thesis rests on an analytics-only product with no paying customer, no price point and no accuracy benchmark.
- No current org chart, raise terms, valuation or verified headcount. 70 FTEs against an unverified 33 suggests possible cuts.

**Terms:** No term sheet at this time. I would revisit only on a small, insider-led, milestone-tranched round. That would be roughly $3-4M in two tranches at a valuation set off verified ARR net of Massachusetts, not the unverified ~$126.8M database figure. It would carry standard 1x non-participating preference and pro-rata rights. Tranche 2 would release only after at least one paid analytics-only contract on samples Biobot did not test, plus a contract-level ARR roll audited by us.

**Would change my mind:**

- A contract-level ARR roll proving Massachusetts was outside the $14.384M book, with no single customer above about 10% of ARR.
- A renewal calendar plus a funding-source tag for all 89 contracts, showing grant-exposed ARR below 20% and a track record of converting grants to budget lines at expiry.
- At least one named customer paying for forecasting or substance-use analytics on samples Biobot did not test, with data-aggregation rights.
- Evidence that state-tier customers renewed against in-house or cheaper bids at held prices.
- A current org chart naming a proven commercial and renewals leader, verified headcount, and clear founder status.

**Checked claims the decision relies on:**

- `1f3fc5752d73-t3-c1` Biobot has $14.384M contracted run-rate ARR across 89 active paying programs as of 2026-09-30.
- `1f3fc5752d73-t3-c4` Biobot runs wastewater-based public-health surveillance.
- `1f3fc5752d73-t4-c8` On an ARR-to-ARR basis, growth from 2025-12-31 to 2026-09-30 is about 41% over nine months, not 62%.
- `1f3fc5752d73-t4-c17` Chunk 09 reports 70 FTEs at FY2025 year-end.
- `1f3fc5752d73-t4-c18` Growjo lists the company at 33 employees (unverified, dated 2026-10-22).
- `1f3fc5752d73-t8-c16` 870 US/Canada cities were identified in 2017.
- `1f3fc5752d73-t11-c9` The synthetic planning model describes itself as 'not observed demand or a total addressable market' (scenario_02).
- `1f3fc5752d73-t16-c2` The ARR bridge to 2026-09-30 shows $280K of churned ARR.
- `1f3fc5752d73-t18-c16` Gross dollar retention on opening ARR was 97.3%.
- `1f3fc5752d73-t18-c17` Net dollar retention was 113.9%.
- `1f3fc5752d73-t21-c1` The fact-check found that buyers beyond municipal government are documented, including pharma.
- `1f3fc5752d73-t23-c14` Due-cohort logo renewal has trended at roughly 90-94%.
- `1f3fc5752d73-t24-c4` Of the 33 contracts renewed in Biobot's 2026 due cohort, 24 renewed on a dedicated budget line and 9 renewed on time-limited grant money.
- `1f3fc5752d73-t24-c7` Municipal and county buyers of Biobot use surveillance or opioid-response appropriations.
- `1f3fc5752d73-t24-c10` Only 24 of the 35 due contracts, or 68.6%, renewed on a recurring budget line.
- `1f3fc5752d73-t33-c5` Implied price is about $676 per scheduled sample, inclusive of dashboards and coverage.
- `1f3fc5752d73-t33-c13` All-in cost is about $288 per sample.
- `1f3fc5752d73-t33-c14` Run-rate service gross margin is 57.4%.
- `1f3fc5752d73-t41-c1` The company operates an 886-site sampling network.
- `1f3fc5752d73-t43-c3` Biobot holds one issued sampling patent, US11714030B2, 'Modular system for upstream wastewater sampling', with Biobot as assignee.
- `1f3fc5752d73-t43-c5` Patent US11714030B2 protects a specific claim set for upstream sampling hardware and does not give exclusivity over wastewater surveillance.
- `1f3fc5752d73-t43-c7` Biobot's founding thesis in 2017 was to differentiate on analytics, not proprietary technology.
- `1f3fc5752d73-t43-c17` WastewaterSCAN runs nearly 150 sites and more than a dozen pathogens.
- `1f3fc5752d73-t43-c18` WastewaterSCAN is philanthropically funded, has Verily as lab partner, and feeds data to CDC.
- `1f3fc5752d73-t48-c5` MWRA says Biobot testing ended at the end of July 2026 as the state moved toward in-house testing.
- `1f3fc5752d73-t48-c6` The Boston Globe reported on 2026-07-04 that the expiring MWRA contract was $400,000 per month.
- `1f3fc5752d73-t48-c7` The Boston Globe reported that the replacement in-house testing was expected to cost about one-quarter as much as the expiring contract.
- `1f3fc5752d73-t48-c13` Government contract concentration and in-house substitution risk are flagged as risks for the company.
- `1f3fc5752d73-t48-c15` MWRA still publishes historical wastewater data, so the history is not locked to the company.
- `1f3fc5752d73-t53-c2` As of 2026-09-30 (synthetic), the median signed-contract sales cycle is 163 days for municipal or county buyers.
- `1f3fc5752d73-t53-c3` As of 2026-09-30 (synthetic), the median signed-contract sales cycle is 318 days for state or federal buyers.
- `1f3fc5752d73-t56-c7` Some paid pilots last year ended without an annual agreement.
- `1f3fc5752d73-t78-c2` CDC names Verily as the current national wastewater testing contractor.
- `1f3fc5752d73-t78-c3` CDC names Biobot as the previous national wastewater testing contractor.
- `1f3fc5752d73-t78-c5` Verily's 2026 CDC award combines lab testing, analytics, pathogen sequencing and possible metagenomics.
- `1f3fc5752d73-t78-c19` Biobot offers substance-use and opioid wastewater programs.
- `1f3fc5752d73-t78-c28` Biobot lost the CDC national contract to Verily.
- `1f3fc5752d73-t83-c4` Biobot had a 2026 ONDCP contract.
- `1f3fc5752d73-t83-c5` Biobot had a 2021 HHS selection.
- `1f3fc5752d73-t88-c4` Newsha Ghaeli's background was in architecture.
- `1f3fc5752d73-t88-c8` On Underworlds, Ghaeli managed the overall effort and owned the urban work: mapping, implementation and interfacing with government workers.
- `1f3fc5752d73-t88-c9` Within her first week at MIT, Ghaeli met Mariana Matus, and the two were charged with making Underworlds a reality.
- `1f3fc5752d73-t91-c1` In 2017 Matus said the Biobot team needed either a mechanical engineer or someone to help on the business side.
- `1f3fc5752d73-t91-c2` Ghaeli described her own strength as building relationships with mayors and executive officers.
- `1f3fc5752d73-t98-c6` As of 2026, Biobot markets disease forecasting and substance-use intelligence beyond COVID.
- `1f3fc5752d73-t98-c16` Rivals, universities and public labs all conduct wastewater surveillance.
- `1f3fc5752d73-t98-c17` In 2017 Biobot's founders were told they had no defensible IP.
- `1f3fc5752d73-t113-c5` Per the synthetic packet, operating burn is about $4,039,560 per year, or $336,630 per month.
- `1f3fc5752d73-t113-c6` Per the synthetic packet, unrestricted cash is $12.8m.
- `1f3fc5752d73-t113-c7` The packet calculates roughly 38 months of runway.
